Csikszentmihalyi’s work led to countless studies about mindfulness and the impact of emotions on results. Other researchers have since suggested that recalling emotions, such as feeling grateful or peaceful, helps to enter a flow state.

Based on studies of emotional intelligence, we know all emotions can trigger the biochemical changes that direct our thoughts and behavior. To override our physical reactions to emotions such as anxiety, anger, or impatience, we can choose emotions that move us into a flow state, such as feeling happy, content, love, gratitude, compassion, and calm.

When I asked the athletes how they felt when in flow, they said they felt peaceful and confident. One person said he felt fully alive. Most said they hadn’t thought about it before but could see how their emotions were significant to maintaining a flow state. If they fell into fear, they fell out of flow.

The practice of maintaining coaching presence can be enhanced by mindfulness exercises where, throughout your day, you can become aware of what is going on inside your mind and body. Developing mindfulness will help you notice when thoughts and sensations in your body occur and then allow them to float away until your mind and body are peacefully present.

他们所掌握的这种心智状态，让他们“进入状态（in the zone）”——全然临在，失去对时间与焦点之外空间的感知，那一刻除了让身体在当下流畅地运动，别无他物。心理学家 Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi 将这种状态定义为“心流（flow）”。他称之为意识的最佳状态，人们无需思考就能发挥最佳表现。Csikszentmihalyi 说，在这种状态中，我们清楚发生着什么，却“以反射性、本能的方式回应”。⁹ 我们信任自己的感官。思考并非必需。

When you apply mindfulness to coaching, you notice disruptive thoughts and reactions, then choose to breathe, clear your mind, and recall what you want to feel so you can quickly return to being present to the person you are with. You choose to feel curious instead of eagerly wanting to tell someone what they need to do, to be patient instead of leaping to find a solution, and to feel courageously quiet instead of wanting to make someone feel better when they are processing an emotion they didn’t expect to emerge.

Csikszentmihalyi 的研究引发了关于正念、以及情绪对结果影响的大量研究。此后其他研究者提出，回想诸如感恩或平和等情绪，有助于进入心流状态。

The art of staying present while practicing your skills is developmental, meaning it takes time to master. You are overcoming a lifetime of mental habits that might have served you well in other situations but now distract from your ability to coach well. As your skill of staying present matures, so will your contentment with being a coach.

Tip Sheet

Receiving with Sensory Awareness

基于情绪智力的研究，我们知道所有情绪都能触发引导我们思想与行为的生化改变。为了压制我们对焦虑、愤怒或不耐等情绪的身体反应，我们可以选择那些把我们带入心流状态的情绪，例如感到快乐、满足、爱、感恩、慈悲与平静。

Practice these tips for maintaining an open nervous system ready to receive while coaching:

  • Notice when you start thinking and stop it. Your thoughts shut off the energy of your full presence. Breathe your awareness into your body to empty out your brain.
  • Let go of knowing . Instead of quickly assuming you know what people want and why they can’t get it, stay curious. You might be surprised. Remember, you are there to help them think. Receive impartially so they can observe their stories with your reflections and questions and can come to know themselves, their desires, and their detrimental thinking patterns on their own.

当我问运动员处于心流时感觉如何，他们说感到平和且自信。一个人说他感到完全鲜活。大多数人说他们此前从未想过，但能看出情绪对维持心流状态意义重大。如果他们陷入恐惧，就跌出了心流。

  • Release the need to do it right . When you offer summaries of what they say and express, accept their response. If they disagree, go with their interpretation. Be wrong; as they correct you, they are getting a better understanding of what motivated their thoughts and actions.

保持教练临在的练习，可以通过正念练习来增强——在一天中，你随时能觉察自己内在心智与身体中发生的事。发展正念，将帮助你注意到身体中何时出现了思绪与感觉，然后让它们飘走，直到你的身心都平静地临在。

  • Trust what you sense with your heart and gut . I have taught and coached in forty-seven countries. No matter the language, people around the world all have sayings similar to “I should have listened to my heart” and “I should have trusted my gut.” Offer what you sense by sharing the bodily change you witnessed. For example, you might say, “Just now, when you paused and looked away, your energy seemed to drop. Would you share what you just experienced?” Your comment will impact their thinking whether or not they accept the invitation.
  • Share patterns you notice . If the flow of the conversation reveals patterns, such as when the client jumps back to talking about the past or declares they want something but keeps coming up with excuses for not committing to changing anything, ask if you can share a pattern you noticed. Share it, and let them respond. Their realization could lead to the breakthrough they need to commit to an uncomfortable but necessary change to achieve their desired outcome.

当你把正念运用于教练，你会注意到扰乱性的思绪与反应，然后选择呼吸、清空心智，并回想你想感受的情绪，从而迅速回归与对话者的临在。你选择感到好奇，而非急切地想告诉别人他们需要做什么；选择耐心，而非急于寻找解决方案；选择勇敢地安静，而非在对方正处理一个意外浮现的情绪时，想让他们感觉好些。

Practice these strategies to clearly receive and share what you observe as your clients’ experience. You may provoke some uncomfortable moments, but that means they are seeing something new. Let them process the new awareness. Stay present but firm so they don’t wiggle away. Your calm firmness shows how much you respect their ability to learn and grow.

在练习技能的同时保持临在，是一门发展性的艺术，意味着它需要时间来精通。你正在克服一身的、或许在其他情境很有用、此刻却干扰你做好教练的心智习惯。随着你保持临在的技能成熟，你作为教练的满足感也会成熟。

exercise: quickly returning to presence and flow

Use this four-step presencing routine anytime you are distracted by your thoughts and emotions. It will help you reopen your nervous system to stay present. 10

当你被自己的思绪与情绪分心时，随时使用这个四步临在练习。它将帮助你重新敞开神经系统，保持临在。¹⁰

The four steps consist of the following:

这四步包括：

  • Relax your body.

- 放松你的身体。

  • Detach your thoughts to clear your mind.

- 剥离你的思绪以清空心智。

  • Center your awareness in your body.

- 把觉察锚定在你的身体中。

  • Breathe in the emotions you want to feel.

- 呼吸进你想感受的情绪。

Step 1: Relax Your Body

You must release the tension in your body before you can clear your mind and shift your emotions. Stress first occurs in your body. Your muscles tighten, your breathing stops or speeds up, your jaw clenches, and your shoulders move toward your ears.

在清空心智、转换情绪之前，你必须先释放身体的张力。压力首先发生于身体。你的肌肉收紧，呼吸停止或加速，下巴紧咬，肩膀耸向耳朵。

Telling yourself to calm down helps for only a few seconds. You must actively shift your biological state before you can control what is going on in your brain.

告诉自己冷静下来，只能管几秒钟。你必须先主动转换自己的生理状态，才能控制大脑里发生的事。

First, focus on your breathing. The quickest way to relieve stress is to inhale for four seconds, hold your breath for five to seven seconds, and then slowly exhale to a count of eight. Repeat this breathing pattern two more times, and then let your breathing return to a normal, easy rhythm.

首先，专注于呼吸。缓解压力最快的方法，是吸气四秒、屏息五到七秒，然后缓慢呼气至八秒的节奏。将这个呼吸模式再重复两次，然后让呼吸回到正常、轻松的节律。

Next, do a quick body scan for any points of tension in your face, neck, shoulders, arms, back, stomach, and legs. Breathe in and relax those spots. If you do only this part of the exercise at various times throughout the day, you will have more energy when you need it.

接着，快速扫描面部、颈部、肩膀、手臂、背部、腹部与腿部，寻找任何张力点。吸气并放松这些部位。如果你一天中只在此时做练习的这部分，需要时就会更有能量。

To sustain relaxation, regularly engage in activities that release tension, such as meditation, yoga, or other calming practices. Do activities that make you smile and feel grateful, such as walking in a park, playing with your children or pets, dancing for fun, or making time in your schedule for your favorite hobby.

为维持放松，要有规律地从事释放张力的活动，如冥想、瑜伽或其他平静的练习。去做让你微笑、感到感恩的事，例如在公园散步、与孩子或宠物玩耍、随性地跳舞，或在日程中为爱好的安排时间。

My favorite form of a quick tension reliever is looking at my “Favorite Pictures” album on my cell phone. The joy and gratitude I feel when I see my family, friends, cat, sunrises from my hikes, and sunsets from my backyard instantly fills my body with a full sense of well-being.

我最喜欢的一种快速解压方式，是翻看手机里“最爱照片”相册。看到家人、朋友、猫、徒步所见的日出，以及后院里的日落时，我所感受到的喜悦与感恩，瞬间让全身充满一种完整的幸福感。

You can also deliberately slow down your life. Eat more slowly, drive more leisurely, and walk at a gentler pace. To consistently clear your mind, lighten up your body.

你也可以刻意放慢生活节奏。吃得更慢、开车更从容、步行更舒缓。要持续清空心智，先减轻身体的负担。

Step 2: Detach Your Thoughts to Clear Your Mind

After you relax your body, free up your mind by detaching the chatter in your brain. Clean out the clutter—your worries about your impact as a coach and troubles outside of coaching. Let your thoughts float away until you feel your peaceful, open mind. You cannot enter the flow state of coaching if your mind isn’t clear.

在放松身体之后，通过剥离脑中的喋喋不休，来解放你的心智。清理杂念——你对身为教练之影响的担忧，以及教练之外的事务。让你的思绪飘走，直到你感到自己平静、开放的头脑。如果心智不清，你便无法进入教练的心流状态。

Start making your practice of detaching a habit by deliberately stopping a few times a day for one minute to let your thoughts float away. You might set a timer so you don’t give in to your chatty brain too quickly.

从每天刻意暂停几次、每次一分钟，让思绪飘走来开始，把这个剥离的练习养成习惯。你可以设个计时器，这样就不会太快向爱说话的大脑让步。

Look around with your quiet mind. Notice the details of the objects in your view. If your mind drifts or you start evaluating or analyzing what you see, smile and let your thoughts float away. Return to noticing the details of the world around you. When you can, increase your time to two minutes, then three, then a glorious five minutes of peace. Each day, see how much longer you can go before you feel you have to go back to thinking.

用你安静的头脑环顾四周。留意视野中物体的细节。如果心智飘移，或你开始评估、分析所见，就微笑着让思绪飘走。回到留意周遭世界的细节。力所能及时，把时间增加到两分钟、三分钟，然后是美妙的五分钟宁静。每一天，看看在觉得自己必须回到思考之前，你能坚持多久。

Step 3: Center Your Awareness in Your Body

Many Eastern philosophies teach that the true center of the mind lies in the center of the body. To get there, you move your awareness out of your head and down into your core, focusing on a spot just below your navel. This shift in awareness is an act known as centering. Athletes, public speakers, and martial artists are taught they are most powerful and courageously in control when they move and speak from their center.

许多东方哲学教导，心智真正的中心位于身体的中心。要抵达那里，你把觉察移出头部、向下进入核心，聚焦于肚脐正下方的一点。这种觉察的转移，是一种被称为“居中（centering）”的行为。运动员、演说者与武术家都被教导：当他们从中心出发去行动与言说时，最具力量、也最为勇敢地在掌控之中。

You can find your center by taking in a deep breath so it fills your belly. Put your hand at the bottom of your inflated belly. Keep your hand there as you let your breath out.

你可以通过深吸一口气、让它充满腹部，来找到你的中心。把手放在膨胀的腹部底端。呼气时，手留在那里。

This spot is also known as your point of strength. Move and speak from this point, and everyone around you will sense your high level of confidence.

这一点也被称为你的力量之点。从这个点出发去行动与言说，周围所有人都会感知到你高水平的自信。

Once your awareness is in the center of your body, recall a moment in your life when you stood or spoke up despite your fears. Remember the courage you had to muster, then notice the strength emanating from the center of your body. Feeling courageous comes from your gut.

一旦你的觉察处于身体中心，回想你生命中曾不顾恐惧而挺身或发声的时刻。记住你不得不鼓起的勇气，然后留意从身体中心散发出的力量。勇气之感来自你的胆识。

At least once a day, take a few moments to close your eyes, breathe deeply, and notice the center of your body. Keep your awareness there for as long as you can.

每天至少一次，花片刻闭上眼睛、深深呼吸，并留意身体的中心。尽可能长久地把觉察留在那里。

When you are comfortable keeping your awareness out of your head and at your center, add a variety of activities to your practice. Play sports, read, listen to music, or hike while focusing your awareness on the center of your body. From this new perspective, you’ll begin to see and hear more details. You will move with both strength and calmness.

当你能自在地把觉察移出头部、停留在中心时，就在练习中加入各种活动。一边把觉察聚焦于身体中心，一边做运动、阅读、听音乐或徒步。从这个新视角，你将开始看见并听见更多细节。你会带着力量与平静一同移动。

Then, take this act of centering into your social interactions. Speaking and listening from your center builds rapport with others. They will feel more connected to you, and they will hear what you say more clearly.

然后，把这种居中的行为带入你的社交互动。从中心出发去说与听，能与他人建立默契。他们会感到与你更有连接，也会更清晰地听到你说的话。

Whenever you are having difficulty staying present, place one hand lightly on your belly and tap your fingers while you slow your breathing. This brings your attention out of your head and into your body.

每当你难以保持临在，把一只手轻轻放在腹部，一边放慢呼吸一边用手指轻敲。这会把你的注意力从头部带出、进入身体。

As with all new habits, centering requires daily practice to become a natural response instead of a technique to remember. Give yourself time to master this important life skill.

与所有新习惯一样，居中需要每日练习，才能成为自然的反应，而非需要记住的技巧。给自己时间去精通这项重要的人生技能。

Step 4: Breathe in the Emotions You Want to Feel

After relaxing, detaching, and centering, choose one or two emotions you want to feel while coaching. Which emotions will help you open your head, heart, and gut? Consider the person you are about to address. Choose two emotions that would help you stay centered on the person, such as calm and compassionate or curious and courageous.

在放松、剥离与居中之后，选择一两种你在教练时想感受的情绪。哪些情绪能帮助你敞开头脑、心与胆识？想想你即将面对的人。选两种能帮助你保持以对方为中心的情绪，例如平静与慈悲，或好奇与勇敢。

Before I coach, I breathe in the feelings “curious and care.” If I get flustered or feel the urge to advise, I breathe in and feel curious and care for my client. The right words always show up.

在我教练之前，我会呼吸进“好奇与关怀”的感受。如果我慌了，或涌起给建议的冲动，我就呼吸并感受对客户的好奇与关怀。恰当的话语总会浮现。

Chapter 8

CHAPTER 5 CATCH AND RELEASE JUDGMENT

We cannot let our ideas blind us to our unknowing.

我们不能让我们的观念，令自己对无知视而不见。

—J ANE H IRSHFIELD

——简·赫斯菲尔德（JANE HIRSHFIELD）

I WAS DOING a coaching demonstration in Beijing, China, in front of a large audience. My client was the vice president of human resources for a large Chinese company. He wanted to explore if he should become a coach when he retired.

我曾在北京的一场大型观众面前做教练示范。我的客户是一家大型中国公司的人力资源副总裁。他想探索自己退休后是否该成为一名教练。

I asked him what he loved about his current job. He told me he loved developing people. He loved seeing the light in their eyes when they realized they could be so much more than they had imagined. Most of all, he loved sharing the principles of Communism with them.

我问他，目前工作中他热爱什么。他说他热爱培养人。他热爱看到人们眼中那束光——当他们意识到自己可以远比想象中更出色时。最重要的是，他热爱与他们分享共产主义的原则。

My body tensed up at a spot just below my rib cage. I had been brought up in a time and culture that taught children in school to prepare for a Communist attack. Never mind they meant Russia, not China, the word triggered a memory emblazoned by fear in my brain.

我的身体在肋骨正下方某处绷紧了。我成长于一个在学校里教导孩子防范共产主义攻击的时代与文化。且不论他们指的是俄罗斯而非中国，这个词触发了我大脑中因恐惧而铭刻的记忆。

I looked at the man who loved seeing the light of possibilities in people’s eyes so much that he wanted to continue to empower others after he retired from his position. I remembered my purpose. Political values had no place in this conversation. I quickly breathed into the tight spot in my body, relaxed, and returned to coaching.

我看着这位男士——他是如此热爱看到人们眼中可能的光，以至于想在退休后继续赋能他人。我想起了自己的目的。政治价值观在这场对话中没有位置。我迅速对体内那个紧绷的点呼吸，放松，并回到教练中。

In the debrief, I was relieved that no one seemed to notice my moment of judgment, but that moment taught me a critical lesson: if I can catch a judgmental reaction in my body, I can release it before it disrupts my presence and affects what I say next.

在复盘时，我庆幸似乎没人注意到我那一瞬的评判，但那一刻给了我关键的一课：如果我能捕捉到身体中评判性的反应，就能在它破坏我的临在、影响我下一句话之前，将其放下。

Judgment is an emotion that shapes your opinions. You have the power to override your judgment by noticing and releasing the tension your body feels when you judge and then choosing what you want to feel instead.

评判，是一种塑造你观点的情绪。你有能力去压制自己的评判——通过注意到并放下评判时身体所感到的张力，然后选择你想感受的替代情绪。

Judgment is your protective brain reacting to what it perceives as a threat, psychological or physical. Your judgy brain also determines what is the safest option of action throughout your day, such as when you enter a crowded room and choose what your brain sees as the safest place to sit.

评判，是你那保护性的大脑在回应它感知为威胁（心理或生理）之物。你那爱评判的大脑，也在全天中决定最安全的行动选项，例如当你进入拥挤的房间，会选择大脑眼中安全的座位。

We are all judgmental by nature. Anyone who says they don’t judge just isn’t aware of when they do. You may be less critical than others you know, but you can’t help being biased and judgmental because your brain says you need to recognize situations that can be hurtful because sometimes they are.

我们天生都是爱评判的。任何说自己不评判的人，只是没有察觉自己何时在评判。你可能比认识的人少些挑剔，但你无法不带有偏见与评判，因为你的大脑说你需要辨认出可能伤人的情境——因为有时它们确实会。

One of the most detrimental yet overlooked emotions we experience is judgment.

我们所体验、却最有害也最被忽视的情绪之一，就是评判。

Judgment often occurs when the brain concludes the people in a situation are acting and saying things that conflict with who we think we are (our identity) or how the world should work (our assumed reality). Even when we are fully present with someone, our brains are discerning what is right, wrong, good, and bad. Then, if what is wrong and bad contradicts our beliefs enough that it feels like an attack, our reactions resemble flight or fight. We either shut down or defensively respond.

评判常在大脑断定情境中人们的言行，与我们所认为的自己（身份）或世界应有的运作方式（我们假定的现实）相冲突时发生。即便我们与他人全然临在，我们的大脑仍在辨别何者对、何者错、何者善、何者恶。接着，如果那错误与不善足够违背我们的信念，以至于感觉像一种攻击，我们的反应便类似战或逃。我们要么关闭自己，要么防卫性地回应。

Because our brain’s priority is to keep us safe, it operates with a negativity bias that may cause us to register even innocent expressions as signs of disrespect or rejection more readily than being neutral or positive. 1 When coaching, your reflections and questions may be skewed by what you unconsciously interpret as negative. You may try to shift the conversation to what you think is a positive perspective without considering their values or desires.

因为我们大脑的优先要务是保全我们，它带着一种消极偏见运作，可能使我们更容易把即便无害的表达，也登记为不敬或拒绝的信号，而非中性或积极。¹ 教练时，你的反思与提问可能被你无意识解读为消极的东西所偏斜。你可能试图把对话转向你认为积极的视角，而不考量他们的价值观或渴望。

Even if your judgment doesn’t seep into your words, your gestures might indicate your displeasure with what was said. Vincent van Gogh wrote, “Don’t let’s forget that the little emotions are the great captains of our lives, and that we obey them without knowing it.” 2 You might lift one eyebrow, scratch your scalp, or restate what your client said but use an upward inflection when you replay the part you doubt or decide is wrong so they feel your judgment. Slight judgmental reactions can impair the trust and safety vital to effectively coach.

即便你的评判没有渗入言辞，你的姿态也可能透露出对所说内容的不满。Vincent van Gogh 写道：“别忘了，那些小小的情绪，是我们生命中伟大的船长，我们在不知不觉中服从于它们。”² 你或许挑起一边眉毛、抓抓头皮，或在重述客户的话时，对你怀疑或认定错误的部分用上扬的语调，让他们感到你的评判。轻微的评判性反应，就能损害有效教练所必需的那份信任与安全。

To catch and release a judgment, you must first accept you are judgmental.

要捕捉并放下评判，你必须先接纳自己是个爱评判的人。

Mastery in coaching requires that you accept you are a judgy, biased person. To judge is human. This is the first step to noticing your judgments before they sabotage your coaching.

教练中的精通，要求你接纳自己是个爱评判、有偏见的人。评判，是人之为人。这是你在对评判破坏教练之前，注意到它们的第一步。

catching a judgment

Some of your judgments are easy to catch; others are so habitual you don’t recognize the disrespect. Whether you catch them or not, judgmental reactions stem from unconscious bias. 3 They often show up when you judge social behaviors such as how loud people talk, how they dress, and how they drive. You might not notice when you offend people by interrupting or ignoring them, even though you dislike when they do this to you. If they call out your reaction, you might tell them you didn’t mean to offend them, but your intention doesn’t matter. They feel slighted anyway.

你的某些评判容易捕捉；另一些则如此习惯性，以至于你认不出其中的不敬。无论你是否捕捉到，评判性的反应都源于无意识的偏见。³ 它们常在你评判社交行为时出现，例如人们说话多大声音、如何穿着、如何驾驶。当你们打断或忽视他人而冒犯对方时，你可能并未察觉——尽管你不喜欢他们这样对你。如果他们点出你的反应，你或许会说自己并非有意冒犯，但你的意图无关紧要。他们终究还是感到了被轻慢。

The proficiency of quickly catching and releasing your judgments takes patience. Regularly practicing the exercise at the end of this chapter will help you create this important mental habit.

迅速捕捉并放下评判的熟练度，需要耐心。定期练习本章末尾的练习，将帮助你养成这一重要的心智习惯。

Because unconscious biases are difficult to uncover, allowing someone else to help reveal them to you is useful. Ask a friend you can trust to kindly point out when one of your biases appears, giving time between instances so you don’t feel attacked. I have a friend who lets me know when I make a snarky remark. As much as I don’t like my judgments pointed out, I am grateful for the awareness. A coach or coaching supervisor can also help you unearth the outdated beliefs behind your judgments.

由于无意识偏见难以发掘，让他人帮你揭示它们会很有用。请一位你信任的朋友，善意地指出你的某个偏见何时出现，并在两次之间留出时间，以免你感到被攻击。我有一位朋友，会在我出酸刻之言时让我知道。尽管我不喜欢自己的评判被点出，我却感激这份觉察。一位教练或教练督导，也能帮你挖掘评判背后那些过时的信念。

catching when you judge emotional reactions

The most common judgmental reactions happen in response to a client’s emotional expression. When you are coaching, even your small show of discomfort will affect the progress of the session. You can easily misjudge the meaning of their movements. Ask if they wouldn’t mind sharing what thought came up that caused the shift in their tone, gaze, or posture so both you and your client come to understand the significance of their expressions.

最常见的评判性反应，出现在对客户情绪表达的回应中。当你教练时，即便你微小地流露不适，也会影响环节的进展。你很容易误判他们动作的含义。问问他们是否介意分享：是什么念头浮现，导致了语气、目光或姿态的转变，好让你与客户双方都能理解其表达的意义。

Here are some commonly misunderstood reactions:

以下是一些常被误解的反应：

  • ✦ Nervous laughter. Laughter is often seen as making light of a situation, but some people laugh when they feel embarrassed or self-conscious. Yale psychologist Oriana Aragon says nervous laughter is a form of emotional balancing, such as when people cry when they are ecstatically happy or make sarcastic remarks when they are afraid. 4 Instead of asking clients what they think is funny or assuming the laughter means they are ready to move on, question what their laughter means to them in this moment. Say something like “What just came up for you that made you laugh?”

- ✦ 紧张性的笑。笑常被视为对情境的轻描淡写，但有些人在感到尴尬或难为情时会笑。耶鲁心理学家 Oriana Aragon 说，紧张性的笑是一种情绪平衡的形式，例如人们在极度开心时落泪，或在害怕时说讽刺的话。⁴ 与其问客户觉得什么好笑、或假设这笑意味着他们准备好继续，不如追问这笑在此刻对他们意味着什么。说些类似“刚才是什么浮现，让你笑了？”的话。

  • ✦ Shift in gaze. Looking away or holding a steely stare doesn’t mean clients are resisting you. You may have touched on a truth they hadn’t faced or considered. The shift is often accompanied by moments of silence. Be still and breathe to give them time to process their thoughts. If they don’t offer a statement, share the shift in their gaze you noticed and ask if they would share what thoughts are coming to mind.

- ✦ 目光的转移。移开视线或死死盯住，并不意味着客户在抗拒你。你可能触到了一个他们尚未面对或考量的真相。这种转移常伴随着沉默的时刻。保持静止并呼吸，给他们时间处理思绪。若他们不主动表态，就分享你所注意到的目光转移，并问他们是否愿意分享脑中浮现的念头。

  • ✦ Easy, quick agreement. If you have a client that often responds to your reflections with something like “You’re right” or “Yes, I get it,” be careful not to accept the response as conclusive. Like nervous laughter, clients might be trying to avoid an uncomfortable idea. Ask them what they think is right about what you said or if they would share what they now understand.

- ✦ 轻易、快速的同意。若你的客户常这样回应你的反思：“你说得对”或“是的，我懂了”，要小心别把回应当作定论。如同紧张性的笑，客户可能在试图回避一个令人不适的想法。问他们，认为你说的哪部分是对的，或是否愿意分享他们现在理解了什么。

  • ✦ Tears . Crying doesn’t always mean someone feels hurt or sad. Tears could be a physiological result of extended periods of stress or a buildup of disappointments that finally bursts out, accompanied by anger. Generally, this reaction will dissipate. Your best response is to sit with compassionate silence as they process their reaction. Do not try to make them feel better. Your attempt to save them or jump to get them a tissue without asking means you see the moment as a show of weakness. They are probably already judging themselves for crying. If they say, “I’m sorry,” respond with “Take your time.” Then, calmly wait for a gesture that suggests they are ready to move on. If they stay silent even when they have relaxed, ask, “Would you be willing to share with me what is on your mind now?” Do not offer to reschedule unless the crying is uncontrollable or they ask for some time to think on their own.

- ✦ 眼泪。哭泣并不总是意味着某人感到受伤或悲伤。眼泪可能是长期压力、或积压的失望终于爆发（伴以愤怒）的生理结果。通常，这种反应会消散。你最好的回应，是带着慈悲的沉默静坐，让他们处理自己的反应。不要试图让他们感觉好些。你试图拯救他们、或未经询问就跳去拿纸巾，意味着你把这一刻视为软弱的表现。他们大概已经在为自己的哭泣评判自己。如果他们说“抱歉”，回应以“慢慢来”。然后平静地等待一个表明他们准备好继续的姿态。若他们即便放松了仍保持沉默，就问：“你愿意和我分享现在心里在想什么吗？”除非哭泣失控、或他们要求独处思考，否则不要提议改期。

  • ✦ Defensiveness. Your reflections can trigger defensiveness when clients don’t like what they hear, even if you are summarizing their words. People don’t like to feel they did something wrong. They might rationalize why they are acting a certain way to make what they are doing right for their situation. If so, reflect their reasoning by saying, “Okay, so your reason for acting this way is . . .” and accept their response. They will think about the moment later in the session or after it is over because you activated the awareness. Stay calm and compassionately curious so your statements don’t feel like arguments.

- ✦ 防卫。你的反思可能触发防卫，当客户不喜欢听到的话——即便你只是在总结他们的言辞。人们不喜欢觉得自己做错了什么。他们可能为“自己为何如此行事”找理由，以使其在自己情境中成为“正确”。若如此，通过说“好吧，所以你这么做的理由是……”来反思他们的推理，并接纳其回应。因为是你激活了这份觉察，他们会在环节进行中或结束后想到这一刻。保持平静与慈悲的好奇，使你的陈述不像是争论。

  • ✦ Hesitation. When a client isn’t fully committed to taking an action, phrases like “I’ll try” or “I guess so” imply their reluctance. Don’t assume they don’t want to take the next step. They may still fear unknown consequences, have concerns over how others will judge their choices, or worry about the effect a change will have on their identity, wondering, “Who will I be if I do this?” 5 Reflect the gesture that indicated their hesitation and ask what is affecting their commitment. They might reveal something new to be addressed. If they stumble when responding, you might firmly ask, “How important is making this change to you?” Sometimes, just speaking the source of their hesitation out loud leads them to committing to the next step.

- ✦ 迟疑。当客户尚未完全承诺采取某个行动，诸如“我会试试”或“我想是吧”这类措辞，暗示了他们的不情愿。不要假定他们不想迈出下一步。他们可能仍恐惧未知的后果、担心别人会如何评判自己的选择，或忧虑改变对其身份的影响，心想：“如果我这么做，我会成为谁？”⁵ 反思那表明迟疑的姿态，并问是什么在影响他们的承诺。他们可能透露出需要被处理的新东西。若他们回应时支支吾吾，你可以坚定地问：“这个改变对你有多重要？”有时，仅仅把迟疑的源头说出口，就引导他们承诺了下一步。

notice how you use “i” in your conversations

How and when you say I when coaching can be helpful or inappropriate. When you use the word before a reflection, such as “I noticed” or “I heard three topics you want to address,” you are offering what you saw or heard for your client to consider. Your use of I is an invitation for your client to think about their words or gestures.

在教练时，你如何以及何时说“我（I）”，可能是有帮助的，也可能不妥。当你在反思之前使用这个词，例如“我注意到”或“我听到你想处理三个话题”，你是在提供你所见或所闻，供客户考量。你对“我”的使用，是邀请客户去思考自己的言辞或姿态。

When you feel the need to share your similar experience to the dilemma your client shared, your use of an I statement shifts the focus away from your client to yourself. You also say I when you want to offer your opinion or suggestion. You detract from your role as a thinking partner when you shift to presenting your analysis, offering your opinion, or giving advice.

当你感到需要分享自己与客户困境相似的经历时，你使用“我”的陈述，就把焦点从客户身上转移到了自己。当你想提出自己的意见或建议时，你也会说“我”。当你转向呈现自己的分析、给出意见或提供建议时，你就减损了自己作为思维伙伴的角色。

Start noticing how you use the word I when coaching to keep your focus on the client. If you refrain from sharing your personal ideas and experiences, you will maintain the flow state of coaching.

开始留意你在教练时如何使用“我”这个词，以保持对客户聚焦。如果你克制住不分享个人的想法与经历，你就能维持教练的心流状态。

Using I appropriately is not an easy habit to develop as you probably have jumped to sharing your own stories in conversations all of your life. Interjecting your I is common when the speaker triggers your memories. If you are a leader or parent, you may think your experiences are critical for others to hear.

恰当地使用“我”，并非容易养成的习惯，因为你大概一辈子都在对话中急于分享自己的故事。当说话者触发了你的记忆，插进你的“我”是很常见的。如果你是领导者或父母，你可能认为自己的经验对别人至关重要、非听不可。

To start noticing when your thinking gets in the way of your presence, try walking around for ten minutes noticing your world without your I getting in the way. See whether you can notice things, structures, and people as if you had never seen them before. What details do you notice? What small things make you smile? What emotions do you notice emerge as you walk around with no thoughts?

要开始留意你的思考何时挡在了临在之前，试着散步十分钟，留意你的世界，而让你的“我”不挡路。看看你能否像从未见过般去留意事物、结构与人群。你注意到哪些细节？哪些小事让你微笑？当你毫无念头地散步时，注意到浮现了什么情绪？

Increase the time from ten to fifteen and then twenty minutes of mindful walking over time. You might notice all the wonderful details you miss when you let your I lead you through life.

随着时间，把正念散步从十分钟增加到十五分钟、再增至二十分钟。你可能会注意到，当你任由“我”领着你度过人生时，所错过的那些美妙细节。

Practice will make you better at maintaining a nonjudg-mental presence, but don’t expect perfection. Moving through life without your I is impossible. But the more you coach from this position, the more you will relax into a not-knowing, open-to-learning state of mind. The breakthroughs your clients experience will come up sooner in your coaching and will likely be more profound.

练习会让你更擅长维持不带评判的临在，但别期待完美。生活中没有“我”的引领是不可能的。但你越是从这个位置教练，就越能放松地进入“不知、向学习敞开”的心智状态。客户所体验的突破，会在你的教练中更早出现，也往往更为深刻。

the judgment in knowing what’s best

Judgment has many faces. In addition to showing up when you react to a client’s belief or emotional expression, a common coaching judgment surfaces when you offer unsolicited advice. You are judging your client as inadequate. Also, when your client starts a session listing multiple concerns, choosing the issue you think should be the direction of the conversation is an imposing judgment.

If you coach because you have learned that people can think more broadly for themselves with a thinking partner, and you desire to facilitate their learning, you must catch the moments when you fall out of partnership and slip into demonstrating you are the one who knows what’s best. Even if you are certain about what’s best for the client, you have to let go of your thoughts so you don’t subtly or obviously lead the conversation to your way of thinking. You cannot be the Expert or Wise One when coaching.

评判有许多面孔。除了在你回应客户的信念或情绪表达时出现，一种常见的教练评判会在你给出未经请求的建议时浮现。你是在评判你的客户有所不足。此外，当客户在环节开始时列出多个关切，你却选择自认为该成为对话方向的问题，这也是一种强加的评判。

I don’t always catch my judgment before I speak. My words and reactions are tainted by my well-developed biases. Sensing when my opinions seep into my words is a lifelong practice I can only hope to improve but not perfect.

I work at shifting back to the partnership after I have drifted a bit. If my opinion is apparent in my question, I quickly add, “Sorry, let me rephrase that.” Then, I attempt to repeat the reflection I made before asking the leading question so my follow-up question represents my curiosity instead of my desire to influence.

Trust your clients can process their thinking and make the right choices without your nudging. Have faith in the power of coaching even when you think the conversation is going nowhere. Let them surprise you. Breakthroughs often show up when you least expect them.

Tip Sheet

Catching and Releasing Your Judgments

Follow these tips to help you develop the mental habit of catching and releasing your judgment:

如果你从事教练，是因为你已懂得：有了思维伙伴，人们能为自己进行更广阔的思考，且你渴望促进他们的学习，那么你必须捕捉自己跌出伙伴关系、滑向证明“自己才是知道什么最好的人”的那些时刻。即便你确信什么对客户最好，也必须放下自己的念头，以免或隐或显地把对话引向你思考的方式。教练时，你不能是专家或智者。

  • Notice when you feel the pinch of judgment. Work on discovering how the emotion of judgment shows up in your body so you can catch it before it infiltrates your thoughts and words. You then have the ability to override your judgment by choosing what you want to feel instead, such as compassionate and curious.
  • Don’t criticize yourself for judging. You will have instinctual reactions to people because of their looks, age, behavior, political affiliation, religious views, lifestyle preferences, and judgments of you. Judging is the human reaction of your protective brain. Don’t condemn yourself for being human. Forgive yourself and choose what to say next.

我并非总能在开口前捕捉到自己的评判。我的言辞与反应，被我高度发展的偏见所染。觉察到自己的意见何时渗入言辞，是一生的练习——我只能希望改进，却无法完美。

  • Question your assumptions about the value of your coaching. Most new coaches question the value of coaching if they can’t offer advice. Leaders think they are useless if they don’t tell people what they should do. It takes time to give up the need to be seen as smart, wise, and useful. The more you coach without giving advice, the more chances you have to witness the power of coaching. Your brain will eventually support your faith that you are providing greater value with coaching than telling.

当我偏移了一点、又飘回伙伴关系时，我会努力修正。如果我的问题中明显带有我的观点，我会迅速补一句：“抱歉，让我换个说法。”然后，我尝试重复在引导性问题之前所做的反思，好让我的后续问题代表的是我的好奇，而非我想施加影响。

  • Strive to be more curious about people every day. In all of your interactions, practice looking beyond what you already know so you can discover something new. When you meet people whose words or actions trigger your judgment, remember they are acting out of their frames constructed by old beliefs, fears of what bad things will hurt them or wreck their lives, and inherited shoulds they think they must live by. You don’t have to agree with their points of view, but you can openly listen to, understand, and accept their perspective as how they see things today. Be careful not to judge the judgers. Choose peace of mind over proving you know better.

We are all judgy judgers. And we all have the capacity to catch our judgments and set them aside when coaching. Model this behavior as often as you can. Maybe we can increase the opportunities for civil discourse in our world.

信任你的客户能处理自己的思考，并在没有你推一把的情况下做出正确选择。即便你认为对话正走向毫无进展，也要对教练的力量有信心。让他们惊喜你。突破常在最不经意时现身。

exercise: going on a judgment field trip

Besides improving your coaching, if you would like to have more peace in your life and improve the quality of your relationships, find days to practice catching the tension caused by your interpretation of other people’s actions and words. The practice increases your self-awareness of judgmental moments, allowing you to release the tension and shift your emotional state to think differently about the situation. Create a plan to follow these steps:

除了提升你的教练水平，如果你想在生活中获得更多平和、并改善关系的质量，可以找些日子，练习捕捉由你解读他人言行所产生的张力。这项练习增进你对评判时刻的自我觉察，让你能放下张力，并转换情绪状态，以不同方式看待情境。制定一个计划，遵循以下步骤：

  • Go to places where you know your judgment will be activated, such as a busy airport or crowded store, or make a call to a family member who always says something that triggers your defensiveness whether you lash out or keep it to yourself.

- 去那些你知道自己评判会被激活的地方，例如繁忙的机场或拥挤的商店；或给一位总说些会触发你防卫（无论你是爆发还是闷在心里）的家人打个电话。

  • Notice where you feel the point of tension in your body when you begin to feel annoyed or angry. Typically, your judgment will show up in this same spot in your body each time. The quicker you recognize this point of tightness, the faster you can release the tension and choose to feel something else.

- 留意当你开始感到恼火或愤怒时，身体中感到张力点的部位。通常，每次你的评判都会出现在身体的同一处。你越快认出这个紧绷点，就能越快释放张力，并选择去感受别的东西。

  • Before you start a coaching session, practice your presencing routine of relaxing, detaching, centering, and choosing two emotions you want to feel when you sense the point of tension you now recognize as judgment. You might use calm and curious or compassion and courage. Choose the emotions you believe will offset your judgment and reopen your nervous system.

- 在开始教练环节之前，练习你的临在例行法——放松、剥离、居中，并在你认出那个如今已知是评判的张力点时，选择两种你想感受的情绪。你可以用平静与好奇，或慈悲与勇气。选那些你认为能抵消评判、并重开神经系统的情绪。

  • When coaching, notice when you feel any points of tightness in your body. Breathe your chosen emotions into the tightness so you can relax and return to being present. After you part with your client, see if you can recall any moments when a judgment showed up to fortify your self-awareness and emotional regulation.

- 教练时，留意身体中是否出现任何紧绷点。把你所选的情绪呼吸进紧绷处，好让自己放松并回归临在。在与客户分别后，看看你能否回想起评判浮现的任何时刻，以强化你的自我觉察与情绪调节。

Remember, you are seeking to be more aware of your reactions. With practice, you will create a mental habit of catching your physical reactions that indicate judgment and shift to the emotional state that helps you stay present. Then you will better appreciate the journey other people are on as they try to do their best to survive and thrive.

记住，你追求的是对自己反应更多的觉察。通过练习，你将养成一种心智习惯：捕捉表明评判的身体反应，并转换到有助于你保持临在的情绪状态。于是你会更好地欣赏他人在努力求生存、求兴盛的旅程。

Part 9

Part III The Five Essential Coaching Skills

It is always better to ask a question than pretend you understand.

提出问题总好过假装自己已经理解。

—A NONYMOUS

——佚名

M ANY COACHING MODELS require the coach to conform to prescriptive, linear steps. Coaches work too hard to meet the requirements and often rush to find solutions with these behaviorally restrictive models. These frameworks can be useful when someone simply needs help identifying possible ways to solve a problem and then sorting through consequences and rewards of each option. The result can be a realistic action plan.

许多教练模型要求教练遵循规定性的、线性的步骤。教练为了符合要求而过于卖力，常常会借助这些带有行为限制的模型仓促寻找解决方案。当某人只是需要帮助找出解决问题的可能方法、进而厘清每个选项的后果与回报时，这些框架是有用的。其结果可以是一份切实可行的行动计划。

These models and conversations are transactional. They focus on alternative actions. They are most beneficial when working with verbal processors—people who need to think out loud—and they feel safe talking through their problems and possibilities with you. I have had these conversations at times with clients; being a sounding board helps when there is no one else they can trust when they just want to sort out their thoughts. But this rarely happens for me. I prefer to participate in conversations as a transformational coach, working on problems with more emotional attachment and murky options.

这些模型与对话是交易型的。它们聚焦于替代性行动。当面对"语言型处理者"——那些需要把想法说出来才能思考的人——并且他们感到与你讨论问题与可能性很安全时，它们最为有益。我有时也和客户有过这样的对话；当他们只想理清自己的想法、却又无人可以信任时，充当一个倾诉板是有帮助的。但对我来说这很少发生。我更愿意以转化型教练的身份参与对话，处理那些带有更多情感羁绊与模糊选项的问题。

You can shift even verbal processors to a more transformational approach when you coach others to identify what is making this situation difficult to resolve or, in the case of making a decision or plan, what has gotten in the way of doing it on their own. Can you discover how their beliefs and perspective are contributing to the hesitation they are facing? When you transform how they think about a situation, they might see the possible actions, risks, and rewards in a new light.

即便面对语言型处理者，当你引导他人去辨识是什么让这个情境难以解决，或者在决策或制定计划时，是什么阻碍了他们独立行事，你也能将他们转向更具转化性的方法。你能否发现他们的信念与视角是如何加剧他们所面临的犹豫的？当你转变他们对一个情境的思考方式，他们或许会以崭新的眼光看待可能的行动、风险与回报。

The vital steps of transformational coaching are to (1) spend time clarifying what the client wants to create or change as a result of coaching around the perceived problem, (2) use curiosity to reveal what blocks need to be addressed or complications need to be processed to achieve what clients desire, and (3) ensure insights throughout the conversation are clearly articulated to easily get a commitment to action at the end of the session to ensure progress.

转化型教练的关键步骤是：（1）花时间厘清客户围绕所感知的问题，想要创造或改变什么；（2）运用好奇心揭示需要处理的阻碍、或需要梳理的复杂状况，以实现客户所渴望的；（3）确保整场对话中的洞见都被清晰表达，从而轻松地在会话结束时获得行动承诺，以保证进展。

These three intentions—clarify the desired outcome, discover what needs to be addressed to achieve the outcome, and highlight insights to move toward a fulfilling commitment to action—provide for both focus and movement in the coaching conversation. Incorporating and practicing achieving these objectives until they easily flow in a conversational style are the essential skills for coaching.

这三个意图——厘清期望成果、发现需要被处理以达成成果之事、并凸显洞见以迈向令人满意的行动承诺——为教练对话提供了聚焦与推进。将这些目标的达成融入并反复练习，直到它们能轻松地以对话风格自然流淌，便是教练的核心技能。

coaching can be both transactional and transformational

Transactional coaching around external problems is useful, especially with a client new to coaching. You might tread lightly below the surface, inquiring about how their beliefs and assumptions are impacting their ability to find solutions to their problem. They can still have significant insights about how their thinking has sabotaged their creativity and confidence. Your facilitation of problem-solving is helpful.

围绕外部问题的交易型教练是有用的，尤其是面对初次接受教练的客户时。你可能会在表面之下谨慎试探，探询他们的信念与假设如何影响他们找到问题解决方案的能力。他们仍能获得重要洞见，看清自己的思维如何破坏了创造力与自信。你对解决问题的引导是有帮助的。

Clients will often thank you for the clarity they gain with transactional coaching. They might not need to delve into their thought patterns, biases, and emotional filters. If the problem is not recurring, a simple reflection or question can lead directly to what action they want to take next.

客户常常会为交易型教练带给他们的清晰而向你道谢。他们未必需要深入探究自己的思维模式、偏见与情绪过滤器。如果问题并非反复出现，一个简单的反思或提问就能直接引出他们下一步想采取的行动。

I had a client in our third month of coaching that said she was so overwhelmed she was questioning her purpose for staying in her job. I asked her, “What is different today from the purpose you felt when you took this job?”

我有一位客户，在我们教练的第三个月时说，她感到不堪重负，以至于开始质疑自己留在这份工作中的意义。我问她："与当初接受这份工作时你所感受到的意义相比，今天有什么不同？"

After a long pause, she said, “That’s it. I haven’t lost my purpose, I forgot it.” She then told me about the dream she shared with her husband about opening a retail store. She took her current job as research to learn the ins and outs of the retail business.

长时间的停顿后，她说："就是这样。我没有失去我的意义，我只是忘了它。"接着她向我讲述了她和丈夫共同拥有的开一家零售店的梦想。她把目前的工作当作研究，用以了解零售业务的来龙去脉。

She then said, “I have to talk to my husband tonight to see if we still share this dream. I’ll let you know what he says. Now, I have a different issue that I’d like to look at with you.”

然后她说："我今晚得和丈夫谈谈，看看我们是否还共享这个梦想。我会把他说的告诉你。现在，我有一个不同的问题想和你一起探讨。"

Not every conversation needs to dig into the source of a client’s beliefs and blocks, but even a little dip beneath the surface to clarify what they desire to create and what has held them back is useful. People act on old beliefs without thinking about how they overshadow their motivations to achieve their desires. Simply asking them to say more about their doubts and assumptions can bring these thoughts to light. Clients might see their beliefs as less substantial today, or they may discard them completely as silly old ideas.

并非每段对话都需要深挖客户信念与阻碍的根源，但即便只在表面之下稍作涉入，以厘清他们渴望创造什么、又是什么让他们裹足不前，也是有益的。人们依据旧有信念行事，却不去思考这些信念如何遮蔽了他们实现渴望的动机。只要请他们多谈谈自己的疑虑与假设，就能让这些想法浮现出来。客户今天或许会觉得自己的信念没那么重要，又或者会彻底将其当作可笑的旧观念而抛弃。

Transformational coaching works best when you have clients that go in verbal circles when defining the problem and they are not clear about what they want or what is making them feel stuck. The focus shifts away from options, risks, and rewards to using reflective inquiry to disrupt their current thinking patterns. Offering observations of their words and gestures along with curious questions prompts them to think about their thinking, which then sparks insights that transform how they see the situation and themselves differently.

当客户在定义问题时陷入语言的循环、且不清楚自己想要什么或是什么让他们感到卡住时，转化型教练效果最佳。焦点从选项、风险与回报，转移到运用反思式探询来打破他们当下的思维模式。对他们言谈与姿态的观察，加上充满好奇的提问，会促使他们思考自己的思考，从而激发出洞见，转变他们看待情境与自身的方式。

Transformational coaching is ideal when behavioral change is needed but clients can’t see what to do or may not believe they can succeed.

当需要有行为上的改变，而客户却看不清该做什么、或可能不相信自已能够成功时，转化型教练是理想的选择。

When you use reflective inquiry when you coach, clients observe their stories, beliefs, and fears as if they are laid out in front of them to examine. From this vantage point, the holes in their reasons for not knowing what to do or thinking they can’t change pop into view. Reflecting and asking questions based on what they see may reveal unspoken fears, the shoulds that are holding them back, and what they truly desire that they were afraid to say. How clients see themselves and what is possible morphs as they broaden their perspective and ideas.

当你在教练中运用反思式探询时，客户会像把自己的故事、信念与恐惧摊开在面前审视一般去观察它们。从这个有利位置，他们之所以不知该做什么、或认为自己无法改变的理由中的漏洞，会突然显现。基于他们所见的反思与提问，可能揭示出未说出口的恐惧、阻碍他们的"应该"、以及他们不敢说出的真正渴望。随着客户拓宽视角与想法，他们看待自身与可能性的方式也会随之改变。

Coaching the person’s thinking instead of just examining ways to navigate around the problem is the cornerstone of transformational coaching, yet even in these conversations, a transactional approach can assist clients in deciding next steps. Toward the end of the conversation, if they aren’t sure what to do next or hesitate to commit, you might ask what options are available regardless of the risk. When exploring these options, they may recognize lingering fears that are causing their hesitation. You might ask questions that examine the possible gains and rewards of their options or actions. You might ask what they would do if their worst case scenarios happened. Often, they admit the worst isn’t likely to happen and go more deeply into the reality of the many results that could happen.

教练客户的思维、而不只是审视绕过问题的方法，是转化型教练的基石；然而即便在这样的对话中，交易型方法也能协助客户决定下一步。在对话接近尾声时，若他们不确定下一步做什么或犹豫是否承诺，你可以问：不论风险如何，有哪些可选方案？在探索这些方案时，他们或许会辨认出导致犹豫的挥之不去的恐惧。你可以提出一些问题，审视其方案或行动可能带来的收益与回报。你也可以问：若最坏的情景发生，他们会怎么做？通常，他们会承认最坏的情况不太可能发生，并更深入地看清可能产生的多种结果的现实。

When clients attribute new meaning to themselves and the world around them, their capabilities, limitations, and definitions shift, causing a corresponding change in choices and behaviors.

当客户赋予自身与周遭世界以新的意义时，他们的能力、局限与自我定义都会发生转变，从而引发选择与行为的相应改变。

In the next five chapters, we will explore how to blend transformational and transactional skills to get the breakthroughs that make your coaching effective. I hope you study and discuss with your peers the explanations, examples, and exercises to elevate the impact of your coaching no matter what, if any, coaching school you attended.

在接下来的五章中，我们将探讨如何融合转化型与交易型技巧，以获得让你的教练卓有成效的突破。无论你是否曾就读于某所教练学校，我都希望你研读并与同行讨论这些阐释、案例与练习，以提升你教练的影响力。

Your reflective inquiry toolbox includes the following:

你的反思式探询工具箱包含以下内容：

  • Focus—Shifting to coaching the person, not the problem

- 聚焦——转向教练这个人，而非问题本身

  • Active replay—Playing back the pivotal pieces for review

- 主动重播——回放关键片段以供检视

  • Navigating desire—Clearing the fog to see the road

- 导航渴望——拨开迷雾看清前路

  • Brain hacking—What are you listening for?

- 大脑破解——你在倾听什么？

  • New and next—Coaching insights and commitments

- 新知与下一步——教练洞见与承诺

Whenever you feel stuck in your coaching development, reviewing one of the chapters might remind you of a practice you have forgotten to use. I hope these chapters act as your quick reference resource for many years to come.

每当你在教练成长中感到卡住，重读其中一章或许能提醒你某个已被遗忘的练习。我希望这些章节能在未来多年里，成为你随手查阅的资源。

Chapter 10

CHAPTER 6 FOCUS Shifting to Coaching the Person, Not the Problem

The challenges we face require us to become aware and change the inner place from which we operate.

我们所面对的挑战，要求我们觉醒，并改变我们运作时所由出发的内在之处。

—C. O TTO S CHARMER

— C. OTTO SCHARME

C OACHING SESSIONS GENERALLY start with clients describing a dilemma they are challenged to solve or naming a topic they want to discuss. This is a good place to start but the wrong place to focus the rest of the conversation.

教练对话通常始于客户描述一个他们难以解决的困境，或点出一个想讨论的话题。这是个好的起点，但却是整场对话错误的聚焦之处。

If you believe the person you are coaching has some work or life experiences to draw from in seeking a resolution to the issue presented, then the focus needs to move away from the external problem and onto the person so your coaching provides discovery and growth. Hopefully, when setting up your coaching relationship, you got an agreement that they wanted this from coaching too.

如果你相信你所教练的那个人，在寻求眼前议题的解决之道时，自有其工作或生活经验可循，那么焦点就需要从外部问题移开，转向这个人，好让你的教练带来发现与成长。但愿在建立教练关系时，你已达成共识：他们也想从教练中获得这些。

You want clients to know they aren’t hiring you to solve their problems. They are hiring you to help them think through what is preventing them from knowing what to do, which leads to the answers they are looking for.

你要让客户知道，他们雇你不是来解决问题的。他们雇你，是帮你理清是什么阻碍了他们知晓该做什么，而这会引导他们找到自己寻觅的答案。

Remember, your clients are smart and resourceful humans. They could probably figure out what to do if layers of thinking patterns weren’t clouding their view. Maybe they are afraid to act until they are certain of the results, as if they ever could be. They might be holding on to a belief that is skewing their interpretation of their situation. They may need someone outside of themselves to surface the fear hiding behind their cynicism, their worries about how they will be judged if they try something new, or their lifelong belief that they will never be good enough. Your job is to expand your clients’ awareness by helping them examine their thought processes.

请记住，你的客户是聪明而机变的人。若非层层思维模式蒙蔽了他们的视野，他们或许本能想出该做什么。也许他们要等到对结果确定后才敢行动，仿佛这确定真能到来。他们或许紧抓着一个扭曲了其处境解读的信念。他们可能需要一个身外的他人，去浮现那藏在其愤世嫉俗之后的恐惧、对尝试新事物将如何被评判的担忧，或那「自己永远不够好」的终生信念。你的工作，是通过帮助他们审视自己的思维过程，拓展客户的觉察。

As they gain clarity from coaching, their awareness and understanding expands. They are then better able to recognize what they need to do to achieve what they desire. They are more likely to commit to actions even when they know their progress may not be easy.

随着他们从教练中获得清明，他们的觉察与理解得以拓展。他们便更能辨认出，要实现所渴望的，自己需要做什么。即便知道进展未必容易，他们也更倾向于承诺于行动。

You coach your clients to find their way forward because you believe they can. You facilitate their process of discovery.

你教练客户去找到自己的前路，因为你相信他们能够。你促成他们发现的过程。

Using coaching techniques that challenge and disturb habitual thought patterns expands clients’ perspective beyond what didn’t work and how to fix it. You spark creative activity in your clients’ brains. The changes in their beliefs and subsequent behavior are both sustainable and adaptable. The conversations may feel uncomfortable at times, but the outcomes are remarkable.

运用那些挑战并扰动习惯思维模式的教练技巧，能把客户的视角拓展到「什么行不通」与「如何修补」之外。你在客户的头脑中点燃创造性活动。他们信念及后续行为的改变，既可持续，又具适应性。对话有时令人不适，但成果却非凡。

supporting versus challenging client thinking

Many coaches struggle with shifting the focus from solving the presenting problem to coaching how their clients are thinking. Both coaches and clients are more comfortable focusing on the external problem. Coaches might ask important questions to help clients analyze their perception of the problem, including what factors they think made the dilemma difficult to resolve. They may even coach clients to identify the strengths they possess that could help them find and implement a solution.

许多教练在把焦点从解决表象问题，转向教练客户如何思考时，都倍感挣扎。教练与客户都更安于聚焦外部问题。教练或许会问些重要问题，帮客户分析他们对问题的感知，包括他们认为是什么因素让困境难以解决。他们甚至会教练客户去辨认那些有助于找到并实施解决方案的优势。

Discussing ways a client could work around difficulties and what strengths they can use to decrease losses is useful, but these approaches are not enough. They allow coaches to avoid challenging the clients’ thinking that made the problem seem difficult to resolve. If anything happens after the coaching relationship ends that rekindles their fears, doubts, and resistance, they might fall back into old thinking patterns. Trying to keep the conversation comfortable and positive derails attempts at expanding clients’ awareness.

The best coaches are comfortable making their clients uncomfortable. The discomfort indicates an insight is trying to break through.

讨论客户可以如何绕过困难、又能运用哪些优势来减少损失，固然有用，但这些方式并不足够。它们让教练得以回避挑战客户那「使问题看似难以解决」的思维。若教练关系结束后，有任何事情重新点燃了他们的恐惧、怀疑与抗拒，他们便可能退回旧有的思维模式。试图让对话保持舒适与正向，会偏离拓展客户觉察的尝试。

最优秀的教练，乐于让客户感到不适。这种不适，正表明一个洞见正试图破茧而出。

To be open to learning, clients must experience at least a brief moment of uncertainty. When their brains realize something new, they experience a break in knowing. The old perspective dies, allowing the new awareness to emerge. Before the revelation is fully formed, the brain takes a moment to reorganize information, so it can’t do its job of providing meaning. The moment may be brief, causing clients to blankly stare before reacting. The longer it takes to fully reconnect neurons to produce an insight, the greater the discomfort with uncertainty.

若要向学习敞开，客户必须经历至少片刻的不确定。当大脑意识到某样新东西，他们会经验到认知的断裂。旧的视角死去，让新的觉察得以浮现。在洞见完全成形之前，大脑需要片刻重组信息，因而无法履行赋予意义的功能。这时刻可能很短，让客户在反应前茫然 stare。神经元重新连接以生成洞见所需时间越长，对不确定的不适就越强。

When the realization forms, clients may emotionally react to what they are realizing. The dopamine rush that often accompanies rapid recognition could spark laughter. The insight can also be painful. They may cry, feel embarrassed, or get angry for not knowing something they now see as common sense. If you stay patiently compassionate with silence, you allow them to process their experience of learning. As the intensity of their emotions subsides, you can ask if they would be willing to share what thoughts are coming up for them.

Case Study

I was coaching the leader of a division in a multinational corporation composed of an administrative base and regional service divisions. Many of the divisions were once separate companies acquired by and merged into the larger corporation. My client’s division was once one of the acquired companies.

当领悟成形，客户可能对所领悟之物产生情绪反应。常随快速辨认而来的多巴胺涌动，可能激发欢笑。这洞见也可能令人痛。他们或许落泪、感到尴尬，或为自己竟不知某个现在看来是常识的事而恼怒。若你以耐心而慈悲的沉默相伴，你便允许他们去消化那学习的体验。当情绪强度消退，你便可问他们是否愿意分享：此刻浮上心头的，是什么思绪。

案例研究

My client had led his team well through the transition. The following year, his division was the top revenue producer for the corporation. Two years later, after a series of decisions by the top leadership team, my client’s division was struggling to meet its goals. That’s when he hired me to coach him.

我曾教练一家跨国公司的某事业部负责人，该公司由一个行政总部与若干区域服务事业部组成。其中许多事业部曾是独立公司，后被收购并入这家更大的集团。我的客户的事业部，便是其中一家被收购的公司。

Our third session started with my client telling me his division was for sale. He then launched into his usual report of how the members of his leadership team were behaving as they tried to cope with the changes, which was leading to anger, fear, and team conflicts. He suggested we focus our session on the person on his team causing him the most trouble because not much had changed for any of his leaders since the last session.

我的客户曾出色地带领团队度过转型。次年，他的事业部成为集团营收第一。两年后，在最高领导团队一连串决策之后，我的客户的事业部开始难以达成目标。正是在那时，他雇我来教练他。

I asked, “We could focus on one person, but since you said not much has changed for everyone, do you want to focus on one person or step back to look at what is keeping all your team members fixed emotionally, including this one person?”

With some irritation, he replied, “I don’t know. I’m doing my best here. They’ve put me out on a limb and keep sawing away at the branch.”

I said, “Your metaphor suggests you are hanging on for dear life before the limb comes crashing down. You have a history of leading your team through difficulties. What is causing you to feel you are helplessly hanging on this time?”

我们的第三次会话，以我的客户告诉我他的事业部将被出售开始。接着他一如往常地汇报：他的领导团队成员在面对变化时如何表现，这正引发愤怒、恐惧与团队冲突。他提议我们把会话聚焦在团队中那个给他制造最大麻烦的人身上，因为自上次会议以来，他的各位领导者几乎都没什么改变。

He looked down and squeezed his forehead. He closed his eyes and pursed his lips into silence. After a long moment, he sighed and said, “I’ve always been asked to grow my organization. Now I’m holding on, trying to protect what we have before there is nothing left.”

我问：「我们可以聚焦在一个人身上，但既然你说对所有人而言都没什么改变，你是想聚焦在一个人身上，还是退后一步，去看看是什么让包括这个人在内的所有团队成员在情绪上卡住？」

I asked him to explain what he meant by “nothing left” to get a better picture of what he feared was happening. After he explained how the decisions were whittling away at his business, I asked him to tell me the difference in seeing himself as the Developer and now the Protector.

他略带恼火地答道：「我不知道。我在这里已尽力。他们把我架在树枝上，还一直锯着那根树枝。」

Again, he paused before saying, “That’s not me. I can’t protect them. They keep asking me if they should move on. I’ve been ignoring them, which is probably why they are spinning out in our meetings. I don’t know what to tell them because I haven’t faced that question for myself.”

我说：「你的比喻暗示你死命抓着，等着树枝轰然落下。你有带领团队渡过难关的历史。是什么让你觉得这次是毫无办法地死抓着？」

I asked what moving on means for him. He swiftly said, “I’m a traitor. My betrayal would leave them clinging to that limb. I’d be the bad guy who let it fall.”

他低下头，揉着额头。他闭上眼，抿紧嘴唇，陷入沉默。过了许久，他叹了口气说：「我一直被要求发展我的组织。如今我抓着不放，想在尚有余地前保护好我们所拥有的。」

I let him sit with his declaration to see if he wanted to say anything else. He looked up at me, frowned, and shook his head. “I understand,” I said. “You feel responsible for holding up the limb. You are committed to being a good transitional leader, but your goals have always been for growth. Knowing you will be sold or possibly broken into pieces that will be sold, you’re not sure what goal to set with your team, or even for yourself. What would be most useful for you now: to look at what conversation you want to have with your team or get clarity on what you need to do for yourself?”

我请他解释「一无所有」对他意味着什么，以更清楚地看见他所害怕发生的事。在他讲完那些决策如何一点点侵蚀他的业务后，我请他告诉我：把自己看作「开发者」与现在的「守护者」，两者有何不同。

他又一次停顿，然后说：「那不是我。我保护不了他们。他们一直问我是否该向前走。我一直回避他们，这或许就是为什么他们在会上陷入了混乱。我不知道该告诉他们什么，因为我还没面对过这个问题。」

With a sigh of relief, he said, “Yes, I need to buck up and hold a truth-telling huddle with my guys. But I first need to think about how I will answer if they ask me what I’m thinking about doing. I like being the Developer. I want to grow something, but I’ve been scared to tell them I want to go. Honestly, it probably won’t make much difference right now if I’m there or not. I can’t protect them, but they are smart guys. Maybe I’m better off working with them as a team to figure out what’s best for each of us.”

我问，对他而言「向前走」意味着什么。他立刻说：「我是个叛徒。我的背叛会让他们吊在树枝上。我便是那个让树枝落下的坏人。」

He committed to going to his cabin for the weekend to set the agenda for his Tuesday team meeting to discuss what is real and not in their control and let everyone share their thoughts and feelings. “I’ll let them blow off steam. It’s okay if they are angry or afraid. I am too. I think then we can have the real talk. After, I’ll set up one-on-ones to talk with each of them about their best next steps.”

我让他沉浸在自己的宣告中，看是否还想说什么。他抬头看我，皱眉，摇了摇头。我说：「我理解。你觉得对撑住那根树枝负有责任。你致力于做一个好的转型期领导者，但你的目标向来是成长。知道你将被出售，或可能被拆散出售，你不确定该为自己、还是为团队设定什么目标。此刻对你最有用的，是去想你打算和团队进行一场怎样的对话，还是厘清你自己需要做什么？」

I both supported my client through his difficult situation and used reflections with questions to challenge him to look at how his thinking was causing his ineffectiveness. This prompted at least two insightful moments: when he paused to face his fear of betraying his team and when he preferred identifying as a leader who grows people and organizations. When he declared his desire to move on, his brain opened to seeing a way to lead his team through the uncertainty to defining what could be next for them. I acknowledged his desire to find new ways to be the leader who grows others.

He did huddle with his team, who thanked him for being blunt about what was going to happen. They said they could now determine both how to make their own decisions and to better lead their teams who were also paralyzed by the uncertain future.

他如释重负地叹了口气，说：「是的，我需要振作起来，和我的弟兄们开一场说真话的碰头会。但我首先得想想，若他们问我我在打算做什么，我会怎么回答。我喜欢做开发者。我想去培育些什么，但我一直害怕告诉他们我想走。老实说，我现在在不在，或许差别不大。我保护不了他们，但他们都是聪明人。也许我不如作为团队和他们一起，去弄清对我们每个人什么最好。」

Most strong-willed clients respect a coach who will challenge their thinking even when they resist. My clients may call me relentless, but they always say my persistence in getting a response to my questions is right for them. Novelist Paul Murray wrote, “If it’s a choice between a difficult truth and a simple lie, people will take the lie every time.” 1 Because of my willingness to coach my clients to face the difficult truths they had been avoiding, they can then move forward with conviction. I use the mental habits you learned in part 2 to calmly and compassionately hold the safe space as they process their realizations and emotional reactions. I believe in their ability to grow, which makes our relationship one of mutual respect.

他承诺周末去小屋，为他周二团队会议定议程，去讨论什么是真实、什么不在他们掌控之中，并让每个人分享自己的想法与感受。「我会让他们发泄。他们愤怒或害怕都行。我也是。我想那时我们就能谈真正的了。之后，我会安排一对一，和每个人谈他们最好的下一步。」

Remember that your client’s problem is only a story. They created a simulation of reality that is causing fears, resistance, and frustration, while their brains provide convenient rationalizations for continuing their ineffective behaviors. Your courageous coaching opens the curtains to let a breakthrough awareness come to light. Stay calm through the waves of emotions they express.

Transformation is an amazing, though often uncomfortable, process. The saying “The truth will set you free, but first it will make you miserable” is a fitting adage in these moments.

我既支持我的客户度过困境，又运用映照加提问去挑战他，让他看见自己的思维如何导致低效。这至少促成了两个洞见时刻：当他停下来直面背叛团队的恐惧时，以及当他宁可认同自己是一位培育人与组织的领导者时。当他宣告渴望向前走，他的大脑便敞开，看见一条带领团队穿越不确定、去界定他们下一步可能的路径。我肯定了他寻找新方法、成为那位培育他人的领导者的渴望。

Good coaching is both supportive and encouraging as well as challenging and disruptive.

Be willing to challenge interpretations, test assumptions, and share the emotional shifts you notice so your clients learn something new instead of just reordering the ideas they already knew. Their tension means your coaching is working! Keep coaching the person, not the problem, and the right criteria for making critical decisions and next step actions will become clear.

他确实和团队碰了头，团队感谢他坦白将要发生的事。他们说，自己现在既能决定如何做自己的决策，也能更好地领导同样被不确定未来所麻痹的团队。

Tip Sheet

Requirements for Coaching How Someone Thinks

Following these steps will help you coach your client’s thinking. Also, use these steps to help explain how you coach when contracting for the relationship before you start coaching:

  • Set the expectations for awareness-based coaching. When establishing logistics for the coaching relationship over the duration of your contract, discuss what a typical coaching session will look like. Don’t start by asking what they want from you; let them know up front you will not act as their adviser. You might share information, inspiring quotes, and resources that will inform their decisions, but you will primarily act as their thinking partner to sort through what is making a situation or decision difficult. As they think out loud with you, you will coach them to examine what is behind their thoughts and motivations so they can determine the best way to move forward. You might suggest something to read or watch to support their goals, but they shouldn’t expect you to give them answers and actions.

多数意志坚强的客户，会敬重一位敢于挑战其思维、即便他们抗拒的教练。我的客户或许会说我锲而不舍，但他们总说，我对问题追要回应，于他们是对的。小说家 Paul Murray 写道：「如果在艰难的真相与简单的谎言之间做选择，人们每次都会选谎言。」¹ 正因我愿意教练客户去面对他们一直回避的艰难真相，他们才能笃定地向前。我运用你在第二部分学到心智习惯，在他们消化自己的领悟与情绪反应时，平静而慈悲地守着那安全的空间。我相信他们成长的能力，这使我们的关系成为相互尊重的关系。

When clients know you believe in their capabilities and you are wholeheartedly there to help them discover their best answers, they will be willing to accept the discomfort of vulnerability that allows them to recognize their biases, contradictions, and fears. Your unwavering belief in your clients creates the conditions for you to coach the person so learning can occur.

请记住，客户的问题不过是个故事。他们制造了一个现实的模拟，正引发恐惧、抗拒与挫败，而他们的大脑则为延续那无效行为提供方便的合理化。你勇敢的教练，拉开了帷幕，让突破性的觉察得以见光。在他们的情绪波澜中保持冷静。

转化是一个惊人、却常令人不适的过程。「真理必叫你得自由，但首先它必使你痛苦」这句话，在此时刻是一句贴切的箴言。

好的教练，既有支持与鼓励，也有挑战与搅动。

Most likely, the competent people you are speaking to need to expand their limiting views about what is right and wrong, what they cannot do, or what bad things will happen if they act on their desires. They might need to unearth their fear of failure or a skewed sense of obligation that is keeping them stuck. They might also need a boost in confidence to do what they already decided is best to do. This is when they realize that when they clear away their mental distractions with your coaching, they can easily recognize and then commit to a next step or plan. You have successfully shifted the focus from coaching the problem to the person.

要乐于挑战解读、检验假设，并分享你所注意到的情绪转变，好让你的客户学到新东西，而不只是重新排布他们早已知道的想法。他们的紧绷，意味着你的教练正在奏效！持续教练其人，而非问题，于是制定关键决策与下一步行动的正确准则，便会清晰起来。

Chapter 11

CHAPTER 7 ACTIVE REPLAY Playing Back the Pivotal Pieces for Review

The instant we wake the story begins: “Here I am. In my bed. Hard worker, good dad ...” And just like that, with our thoughts, the world gets made.

我们醒来的瞬间，故事就开始了：「我在这儿。在我的床上。勤奋的工作者，好爸爸……」就这样，凭借我们的念头，世界被创造了出来。

—G EORGE S AUNDERS

——George Saunders

W HEN YOU USE reflective statements, you become a dynamic mirror where clients can more objectively view the stories they are living in. You summarize and question the thoughts they spoke out loud, what they hope will happen but doubt, and the beliefs holding them back from their dreams. Hearing their thoughts spoken by the coach can be jarring. This surprise and even discomfort signals the opportunity to coach clients to think more deeply about what they want and what makes them think they can’t get what they want.

当你运用反思性陈述时，你就成了一面动态的镜子，让客户能更客观地审视自己所身处其中的故事。你概述并追问他们大声说出的念头、他们希望发生却又怀疑的事，以及阻碍他们追逐梦想的种种信念。听到教练说出自己的念头，客户可能会感到错愕。这种意外甚至不适，正预示着教练客户更深地思考他们想要什么、又是什么让他们认为自己得不到想要之物的机会。

After earning a master’s degree in broadcasting, I was hired as the audio-visual coordinator for a psychiatric hospital corporation. It was a fancy title for the job of moving around and setting up television monitors, video players, and film projectors. I also operated the video equipment if a patient needed to be recorded on camera. What started as boring tasks turned out to be one of the most fascinating experiences of my life.

在获得广播学硕士学位后，我被一家精神科医院集团聘为视听协调员。这个时髦的头衔，实际工作不过是搬动和架设电视监视器、录像机与电影放映机。如果有患者需要被摄像机记录，我也负责操作摄像设备。起初无聊的任务，最终成了我一生中最迷人的经历之一。

My master’s thesis explored the rise in self-esteem using video feedback where people watched a recorded presentation they gave. The focus was on improving presentation skills. We started by talking about how they ideally wanted to feel when delivering their short speeches. Then, they were recorded three different times, speaking about a topic they were interested in. After each recording, we talked about what they saw they could improve to move toward their ideal delivery.

我的硕士论文探讨了利用视频反馈提升自尊——即让人们观看自己录制的一段陈述。重点是改善演讲技巧。我们先谈他们理想中在发表短演讲时希望有的感受。然后，就一个他们感兴趣的话题，把他们录下来三次。每次录制后，我们都会讨论他们觉得自己可以如何改进，以更接近理想的表达。

Although the first two recordings were uncomfortable to watch, their ability to objectively watch without focusing on how badly they looked increased. After the third session, they were excited about continuing their development. They saw themselves stand straighter, look more directly to the camera, use their hands more intentionally, and speak with more certainty and clarity. Their self-assessments written both before and after the study indicated a rise in self-esteem. Their comments to me about the experience always included the words “more confidence.”

尽管前两次录像令人看得不舒服，但他们不加评判、客观地观看（不再盯着自己表现多糟）的能力增强了。第三次之后，他们对继续发展自己感到兴奋。他们看到自己站得更直、更直视镜头、更用心地运用双手，说话也更笃定、更清晰。研究前后所写的自评都显示自尊有所提升。他们对我说起这次经历时，话语中总少不了「更有信心」这几个字。

I shared my research with a psychiatric nurse working with anorexic patients. She told me when her patients looked in a mirror, they saw themselves as fat. She didn’t think watching videos of themselves would make a difference but thought it would be interesting to try. She asked her attending psychiatrist if he thought the video playbacks would be harmful. He encouraged the experiment as long as he could be present at the recordings.

我与一位照料厌食症患者的精神科护士分享了我的研究。她告诉我，她的患者照镜子时，看到的是自己很胖。她不认为看自己的录像会有什么不同，但觉得试一试会很有意思。她询问主治精神病医生，录像回放是否会造成伤害。只要他能在录制现场，他就鼓励这个实验。

The results of even one recording and playback were amazing. For the first time, the patients we recorded saw how ghostly thin they had become. There were tears but no trauma. They indicated a willingness to better participate in their therapeutic interventions. I didn’t get the opportunity to repeat the exercise because of an organizational restructuring that shifted my responsibilities, but the nurse told me the patients who participated became more actively involved in their treatment.

哪怕只是一次录制与回放，结果也令人惊叹。我们录下的患者第一次看到自己已变得多么形销骨立。有泪水，但没有创伤。他们表示愿意更好地配合治疗干预。由于一次组织重组使我职责变动，我没机会重复这个练习，但那位护士告诉我，参与的患者对治疗的投入变得更为积极。

This led me to realize that instead of just providing a static mirror, using active replay, where people see a dynamic representation of themselves, provoked the self-awareness and self-acceptance necessary for bringing about change. Until you compassionately accept who you are being today and then bravely declare who you can become, the conversation around strengths and actions lacks substance.

这让我意识到：与其只提供一面静止的镜子，不如运用主动重播——让人们看到自己动态的样子——从而激发出改变所必需的自知与自我接纳。除非你带着悲悯接纳当下的自己，再勇敢宣告自己可以成为谁，否则围绕优势与行动的对话就会缺乏实质内容。

Actively replaying your client’s significant words and expressions effectively activates the self-reflection needed for growth. Coaching schools commonly refer to these actions as mirroring skills, but unlike a mirror, you aren’t providing just the surface layer. Your reflective statements and follow-up questions provide an active replay that allows for deeper explorations of the beliefs, fears, disappointments, betrayals, and conflicts of values that hinder your client’s view of what is in their control to move toward a desirable result.

主动重播客户那些重要的言辞与表达，能有效激活成长所需的自我反思。教练学校通常把这些做法称为镜像技巧（mirroring skills），但与镜子不同，你提供的不是表层。你的反思性陈述与追问，构成了一种主动重播，使人得以更深入地探索那些阻碍客户看清「自己能掌控什么去迈向理想结果」的信念、恐惧、失望、背叛与价值冲突。

Two skills are necessary to enhance your active replay abilities: (1) summarizing key words and phrases clients said and (2) reflecting shifts in gestures, postures, and emotional expressions and then asking what happened in that moment. Summarizing and reflecting physical and emotional shifts involves some useful sub-skills described in this chapter.

要提升你的主动重播能力，需要两项技能：（1）概述客户说出的关键词与短语；（2）映照姿态、手势和情绪表达的变化，然后追问那一刻发生了什么。概述并映照身体与情绪的变化，涉及本章介绍的一些有用子技能。

Pairing reflective skills with both clarifying and exploratory questions is the foundation of your practice of inquiry. Your exploration goes deeper into the sources of clients’ thought formation. The disruption of thinking patterns may cause some discomfort but will lead to the clarity and confidence clients need to move forward.

将反思技能与澄清式、探索式问题结合，是你探询实践的根基。你的探索会深入客户念头形成的源头。思维模式的被打断可能引起些许不适，却会带来客户向前迈进所需的清晰与信心。

summarizing

Although summarizing seems simplistic, the effects are powerful. When people hear their own words spoken, their ideas and beliefs are laid out in front of them to examine. They then go inward to reflect on the meaning of their statements and how their thoughts are defining the situation they are exploring. They may glimpse what was a blind spot impacting their beliefs. Concise summarizing alone can lead to a clarifying insight.

尽管概述看似简单，效果却有力。当人们听到自己的话被说出，他们的想法与信念就被摊开在面前供检视。他们随后向内反思自己陈述的含义，以及其念头如何界定他们所探索的处境。他们或许会瞥见那个影响信念的盲点。单单简洁的概述，就能促成一次清晰的洞见。

The intent of summarizing is not to memorize and then parrot back to the clients what they said. The aim is to pull out the repeated or descriptive phrases you heard, using their words, so they can observe their own thinking more clearly. You aren’t clarifying for just yourself; you help your clients clarify what they mean, what they want to retain, and what they see can change.

概述的目的，不是记住客户的话再逐字复述回去。目标是提取你听到的、被反复提及或具描述性的短语，用他们自己的话，让他们能更清晰地观察自己的思考。你不是在只为自己澄清；你帮助客户澄清他们所指的、想保留的，以及他们认为能改变的东西。

They may edit what you say or share deeper thoughts about their statements. Your task is to continue to listen for the key words and ask them to explain what the words mean to them or to challenge what they are presenting. You are seeking to illuminate their story even as it expands and evolves.

他们可能编辑你说的话，或对自己的陈述分享更深层的想法。你的任务是持续倾听关键词，并请他们解释这些词对自己的意义，或去质疑他们呈现的内容。你是在寻求照亮他们的故事，即便它在延展与演化。

The practice of summarizing includes three skills: (1) recapping, (2) paraphrasing, and (3) encapsulating. You will often follow up these statements with a question to confirm the accuracy of your words or to expand the definition, such as “You keep using the word pressure. What is pressure causing you to do?” The follow-up might be “Who is putting this pressure on you?” The summary prompts clients to begin dissecting their stories.

概述这一做法包含三项技能：（1）重述、（2）释义、（3）凝练。你常会在这些陈述之后跟一个问题，以确认你措辞的准确性，或拓展定义，例如「你一直用『压力』这个词。压力让你在做什么？」追问也可能是「是谁把这种压力加在你身上？」概述促使客户开始剖析自己的故事。

Recapping

One of my most used phrases is “So you are saying . . .” Then, I concisely sum up the presenting problem or topic, anything they say they need to do or have right now, and what factors are making it difficult to take action. The client may correct or add to my synopsis whether or not I ask a follow-up question.

我最常用的一个短语是「所以你是说……」。然后，我简明地总结呈现出来的问题或话题、他们所说此刻需要做或拥有的任何事，以及让行动变得困难的因素。无论我问不问追问，客户都可能纠正或补充我的概述。

Although you are highlighting key words they use to paint their picture, don’t leave out a sidenote they speak but then ignore. Side comments such as “I know this is crazy,” “Nobody cares,” or “I wish I could just leave” hint at the major belief that is keeping the client stuck.

尽管你在凸显他们用来描绘图景的关键词，也不要漏掉他们顺带说出却又忽略的旁注。诸如「我知道这很疯狂」「没人在乎」或「我真希望一走了之」之类的旁白，暗示着让客户卡住的核心信念。

Case Study

Case Study

My client was describing her disappointment with her husband for remaining in a night shift job for five years. She thought they had agreed he would seek a day job when they had a child; they now had two children. He deflected her attempt to talk about the situation.

我的客户在抱怨丈夫五年来一直做夜班工作让她失望。她以为两人曾约定，有了孩子后他会找一份白天的工作；如今他们已经有了两个孩子。他回避了她想谈这件事的尝试。

I recapped by saying, “I hear that you want your husband to seek a day job, but he’s not wanting to have this conversation with you.” She agreed. I then asked what she meant by “he deflected.”

我重述道：「我听到你希望丈夫去找一份白天的工作，但他并不想和你谈这件事。」她表示同意。接着我问，她所说的「他回避」是什么意思。

She said with frustration, “A year ago I tried to talk about his getting a day job. He threw up a wall so big I’m scared to bring it up again. Now we don’t talk much about anything important. In the little time we have at home together, he’s busy with things on his phone. He won’t let me in.”

她懊恼地说：「一年前我试着谈他找白天工作的事。他筑起一道好高的墙，我怕得不敢再提。现在我们很少谈什么重要的事。在家共处那点时间里，他忙着看手机上的东西。他不让我靠近。」

I said, “I see. The one time you tried to talk about his job triggered a rift in your relationship, so now, even when you are together, you don’t connect. When you stressed the words threw up a wall so big, was that the moment your relationship changed?”

我说：「我明白了。你那次试着谈他的工作，触发了你们关系中的裂痕，所以现在，即便在一起，你们也不再有联结。当你强调『筑起一道好高的墙』这几个字时，那就是你们关系改变的时刻吗？」

She looked down and frowned. After a long pause, she softly said, “Yes, I’m afraid to touch the wall.” I stayed silent as it felt like she was processing what she revealed. She finally said, “I think my fear is getting in the way of understanding his fears.”

她低下头，皱起眉。长久的停顿后，她轻声说：「是的，我怕去碰那道墙。」我保持沉默，因为感觉她正在消化自己透露出的东西。她终于说：「我想，是我的恐惧挡在了理解他的恐惧面前。」

I asked what the relationship would look like if there were no walls. She said, “Like it used to be when we felt close and talked about everything.” I asked who she sees herself being in that picture. She said, “The wife who loves her husband and trusts he does what he can for his family.”

我问，如果没有墙，这段关系会是什么样。她说：「就像从前那样，我们亲近，什么都说。」我问，在那幅画面里她觉得自己是谁。她说：「那个爱丈夫、并相信他会为家庭尽力的妻子。」

I waited a moment before I asked, “Do you want to explore ways of approaching the conversation about his job or ways of breaking down the wall that you feel is keeping you apart?” She said she wanted to find a way to let him know their relationship was more important than his job. She hoped he too wanted to have a relationship with no walls. With this agreement in place, the coaching moved into how she would approach this conversation both emotionally and verbally while managing her expectations.

我等了片刻，才问：「你想探索如何切入关于他工作的对话，还是想探索如何拆掉那道你觉得把你们隔开的高墙？」她说，她想找个办法让他知道，他们的关系比他的工作更重要。她希望他也想要一段没有墙的关系。达成这个共识后，教练转入了她将如何在管理自己预期的同时，从情绪和言语上切入这场对话。

When recapping, use their words and highlight their metaphors to explore. They will correct you if they meant something else. Include the emotional descriptions they use when they stress their desires, fears, and irritations. Don’t analyze what they offer; you will miss key points they slip in while you are thinking.

重述时，使用他们的话，并凸显他们的隐喻去探索。如果你理解有偏差，他们会纠正你。要包含他们在强调愿望、恐惧和恼怒时用到的情绪化描述。不要分析他们给出的东西；你一思考，就会漏掉他们顺带滑入的关键点。

Recapping to Override Your Assumptions

Too often, coaches hear the presenting stories and think they have the full picture. When you initially recap what you hear, you are just beginning to understand how clients see their situations. Generally, they will add important details after they hear your summary.

教练们常常听到客户呈现出来的故事，就以为自己已经看清了全貌。当你最初重述所听到的内容时，你才刚刚开始理解客户如何看待他们的处境。通常，在听到你的概述之后，他们会补充重要的细节。

When you assume you know what they are experiencing without clarifying and confirming their perspective, you are probably wrong.

当你在没有澄清并确认客户视角的情况下，就假定自己知道他们的体验，你很可能是错的。

As you clarify what they see, mean, and want, your questions should reveal why they feel the need to resolve this dilemma, what keeps coming up in their thoughts that stop them from taking action, and how long they have been stuck in the same place. Capturing these points, offering them back to consider, and asking questions to further clarify meaning and desires helps clients sort out the puzzle pieces of their stories so they can see what is causing the gap between what they want to have happen and what they see as happening at that moment.

随着你澄清他们所看到的、所指的以及所想要的，你的问题应当揭示出：为什么他们觉得有必要解决这个困境、是什么念头不断浮现、阻碍他们采取行动，以及他们被困在原地有多久。捕捉这些要点、把它们交还给客户去思考，并追问问题以进一步澄清其意义和愿望，能帮助客户梳理自己故事中的拼图，从而看清是什么造成了他们想要发生的事与当下所见情形之间的落差。

Be sure to note any statements they make starting with “I want,” “I need,” or “I should.” Explore the importance of their wants, the measure of their needs, and the weight of their shoulds that conflict with what they want. You will then move closer to determining what is possible for them to do to work toward their aspirations.

务必留意客户以「我想要」「我需要」或「我应该」开头的任何表述。探究他们愿望的重要性、需求的程度，以及与愿望相冲突的那些「应该」的分量。这样你会更接近确定他们有可能做些什么，去朝自己的抱负迈进。

Be patient when recapping and clarifying. When you help clients crystallize the picture of their ideal outcome, free from their shoulds and fears, what follows in coaching will be both easier and fruitful.

重述与澄清时要有耐心。当你帮助客户厘清理想结果的图景——摆脱他们的「应该」与恐惧——教练后续的过程就会既轻松又富有成效。

Recapping to Recognize Conflicts and Contradictions

Even if you clarify your clients’ ideal results, they often feel stuck when talking about the expectations others have of them, their sense of having no control over the circumstances, and the external pressures they see as real for them. As you try to compare their ideal scenario with their current perceptions, they grow increasingly frustrated because they think you don’t understand what they must live with.

即便你澄清了客户理想的结果，当谈到他人对他们的期望、自己对环境毫无掌控的感觉，以及他们视为真实存在的外部压力时，他们常常还是感到困住。当你试图把他们的理想情境与当前感知作比较，他们会越来越沮丧，因为觉得你不理解他们必须忍受的一切。

Don’t push them. Keep offering back what you hear. Their resistance could be a sign that they are stepping onto the scary path of transformation that will take courage and time before they can fully see the picture of a desired future.

不要逼迫他们。继续把他们听到的话奉还回去。他们的抗拒，可能正表明他们踏上了转变这条可怕的道路——在能完整看见理想未来的图景之前，还需要勇气和时间。

Case Study

Case Study

I was coaching a woman who wanted to discover how she could enjoy her work more. She said her job had become easy with no challenges. She wanted to start a new project, but the “stupid internal conflicts” took up too much of her time and energy. She couldn’t see a way out of this predicament. She often took out her anger on her family.

我曾教练一位女性，她想弄明白自己如何能更享受工作。她说工作已变得轻松、毫无挑战。她想启动一个新项目，但那些「愚蠢的内部冲突」占用了太多时间和精力。她看不到摆脱这个困境的出路。她常把怒气发泄在家人身上。

I recapped how the “stupid” conflicts were time- and energy-consuming, keeping her from seeking the challenging new projects she wanted and causing stress at home. She defensively told me the job paid well and she held a respected title. Her family benefited in many ways by her having this job. I summarized the reasons for staying there and shared that she tensed up and got louder when she reacted to my reflections. I asked if she was irritated with me for not understanding her situation.

我重述了那些「愚蠢的」冲突如何耗费时间与精力，让她无法去追求想要的有挑战的新项目，并在家里造成压力。她带着防备告诉我，这份工作薪水不错，头衔也受人尊重。家人因她拥有这份工作而在多方面受益。我概述了她留下的理由，并分享说，她在回应我的映照时会紧绷、提高音量。我问她，是否因为我不理解她的处境而恼火。

She burst out, “Am I wrong for wanting to go? Everyone says I should be honored to hold my title. I am supporting my family well. Who knows what will happen to me if I leave?”

她脱口而出：「我想走，难道是我错了吗？人人都说我该为自己有这个头衔感到荣幸。我把家养得很好。谁知道我离开会怎样？」

I said, “You asked if you were wrong for wanting to leave. Are you looking for a way to change your desire to leave or making it okay to leave?”

我说：「你问自己想走是不是错了。你是在寻找改变『想离开』这个愿望的办法，还是在为离开寻找合理性？」

She stared blankly at me. I sat silently. Finally, she said, “I should be grateful, but I don’t feel that way.”

她茫然地瞪着我。我沉默地坐着。终于，她说：「我应该心怀感激，但我不是那种感觉。」

I paused for a moment before saying, “It sounds like your desires are in conflict with what you think you should do, that you are wrong, or even bad, for wanting to seek something else.” She nodded in agreement.

我停顿片刻，然后说：「听起来你的愿望和你认为自己应该做的事相互冲突——你觉得自己错了，甚至因为想要别的可能而觉得自己很糟。」她点头表示同意。

“Just to be clear about where we go from here, I’d like to know how strong your desire is to leave compared to your responsibility to do what is right.” Holding my hands up as if I were a scale weighing something in each hand, I asked her to weigh her desire to leave against the reasons for staying.

「为了清楚我们接下来去向何方，我想知道，相比你做『正确之事』的责任，你想离开的欲望有多强。」我双手抬起，仿佛手里各端着秤盘，请她把想离开的欲望与留下的理由放在秤上掂量。
